see notes","description":"Almost 10 years ago, Black Lives Matter held a sit in, demanding then Executive Director, @mathieu chantelois, agree to ban uniformed police officers from marching in the Pride Parade.\n\nSubsequently, when the Pride organization voted to support the ban, it created deep divisions in the city, and throughout the 2LGBTQ+ community in particular.\n\nAt the time, I took a very public stance.  My view was that there has already been so much taken away from our community, that creating deeper divisions without any clear narrative that would solve anything, was not going to be helpful for the community as a whole and likely was not even going to be helpful in addressing issues of racism.\n\nThe leaders of BLM in Toronto had just faced serious financial impropriety allegations, the primary leader had just went on a microphone publicly calling Justin Trudeau a white supremacist terrorist, amid a litany of other mayhem that might have seemed like great activism to those serving it, but to the average person not only came across as disorganized and at times violent, but also lacked any coherent talking points other than people of colour felt triggered by seeing police in uniform marching in the parade, while they stood a few feet away in full uniform on the sidelines providing city-funded protection to parade goers.\n\nAs I watched the divisions deepening by the hour, with the left launching its usual well-orchestrated politically correct rhetoric, the right firming its stance that nothing was wrong and there was nothing to see or do here except let the police back in, I looked at the situation and realized what ultimately ended up happening, which was that without a clear narrative that made sense, and without some sort of a plan to clean up the divisions, all that was happening was a division that not only fractured out community but also put us front and center to the rest of the city as a community that didn’t know what it wanted, didn’t even know what it wanted to say, and we were willing to go to extremes to be heard about it.  I’ve lost a lot in the process of being outted and coming out, I’ve also lost a lot because of the Church, and I’ve spent a long time learning about myself and learning about my community, enough to know that what Pride was doing, without much transparency, was wrong and it did not represent me.\n\nI met with one of the owners of the bathhouses that was raided, which was one of the major talking points of BLM.  He said he did not support the police being banned at all, even though he was there as the police smashed the bathhouse to pieces in front of his eyes.\n\nHe felt there had been so much work put into the relationship with the police over the years, that the ban was going to set us back in all of that and he didn’t want to see it all be for nothing.  So he and I stood in front of the pride office door one early January morning, holding a sign that said Not my Pride.\n\nWith that, things evolved into a petition of over 10,000 signatures which John Tory ignored, and the attempt to create a space where police were welcome, in or out of uniform, complete with performers from the community including several who are transgender and people of colour, who each volunteered their time to perform and show support for the police remaining in the parade.\n\nI took a lot of heat for organizing this event, there were lots of opinions about it, but my motivation was simply to say I didn’t support setting our community back like this and I am just as much allowed to try to keep things from falling apart as BLM was in trying to deconstruct them.\n\nDon’t get me wrong.  It is easy to assume that by doing this, I didn’t think there was any issue with the police and their treatment of people of colour or minorities in this city.  And it is easy to assume that I didn’t support the goal of BLM with regards to addressing racism and systemic inequalities that are not faced by white and white-passing people (such as myself, even as an Indigenous person, that looks white).  But both are entirely untrue.\n\nIn fact, if the tactics of BLM were not so clearly divisive and held even some iota of a way of uniting people and a narrative that made sense, unformed police triggering a ban necessity not remotely meeting the level of common sense that the vast majority of the city and even many people of colour, felt was necessary to take such drastic steps, then they probably would have had my support.\n\nI wrote to BLM and requested and offered to meet with them, their response was to attack me on Twitter.  I filed a human rights complaint and they attacked that.  It’s fine, there was a certain amount of optics to the entire situation that required arguments to be spelled out clearly since BLM’s script kept changing whenever common sense poked through and their actions weren’t aligning with what they were saying.\n\nBut I think with better leadership they actually could have accomplished quite a bit.  As I said at the time, and I reiterate today, I hope I don’t know anyone who thinks that there isn’t a racism problem because we should all know there is one.  And a lot more work needs to be done.  It will always need to be done because so long as there are hierarchal power structures, there will exist attempts to silence and minimize marginalized groups first. \n\nAnd I have changed my views on a few things in fact, so I wouldn’t say that their efforts were entirely for naught.\n\nOne example was the proposal that a social worker should be in police cars so that we have a professional ready to diffuse situations where police are being put into situations of applying force when it may not be necessary.  At the time I felt it was financially not feasible but I’ve come to realize that it is both feasible but also a very smart idea.  And I believe it would work.  I didn’t at the time.\n\nCould I have been convinced without all the drama?  Probably. I think most people are reasonable enough to consider new ideas when they are presented properly and in this case even when they are presented improperly if the person is open to it.\n\nThe problem is that I forced myself to remain open because I was in a battle that necessitated being sure of my positions or relatively sure and ready to adapt if necessary.  But most of the public neither has the time or interest to do that so if they aren’t given a clear and compelling argument quickly, the message gets lost.\n\nI changed my views on some things.  I’m not sure how many other Torontonians did so though, Pride almost went bankrupt after these dramatic events as supporters, individual and corporate, pulled funding and money in opposition to the decision to ban the police.\n\nI was also looking at some of the cases in the US where people of colour were being savagely beaten by police and the reactions of organizations of BLM, which in fairness really ought know no limits in terms of a response.  If people who aren’t people of colour were living in a community that was being routinely and systemically being treated like this, the response should be and must be harsh and swift.\n\nBut the response can’t turn into financial theft, nor calling our Prime Minister a white supremacist terrorist, when at that same time Donald Trump was president and I think most people would agree he is at least one of those two things, if not both. see notes","description":"Almost 10 years ago, Black Lives Matter held a sit in, demanding then Executive Director, @mathieu chantelois, agree to ban uniformed police officers from marching in the Pride Parade.\n\nSubsequently, when the Pride organization voted to support the ban, it created deep divisions in the city, and throughout the 2LGBTQ+ community in particular.\n\nAt the time, I took a very public stance.  My view was that there has already been so much taken away from our community, that creating deeper divisions without any clear narrative that would solve anything, was not going to be helpful for the community as a whole and likely was not even going to be helpful in addressing issues of racism.\n\nThe leaders of BLM in Toronto had just faced serious financial impropriety allegations, the primary leader had just went on a microphone publicly calling Justin Trudeau a white supremacist terrorist, amid a litany of other mayhem that might have seemed like great activism to those serving it, but to the average person not only came across as disorganized and at times violent, but also lacked any coherent talking points other than people of colour felt triggered by seeing police in uniform marching in the parade, while they stood a few feet away in full uniform on the sidelines providing city-funded protection to parade goers.\n\nAs I watched the divisions deepening by the hour, with the left launching its usual well-orchestrated politically correct rhetoric, the right firming its stance that nothing was wrong and there was nothing to see or do here except let the police back in, I looked at the situation and realized what ultimately ended up happening, which was that without a clear narrative that made sense, and without some sort of a plan to clean up the divisions, all that was happening was a division that not only fractured out community but also put us front and center to the rest of the city as a community that didn’t know what it wanted, didn’t even know what it wanted to say, and we were willing to go to extremes to be heard about it.  I’ve lost a lot in the process of being outted and coming out, I’ve also lost a lot because of the Church, and I’ve spent a long time learning about myself and learning about my community, enough to know that what Pride was doing, without much transparency, was wrong and it did not represent me.\n\nI met with one of the owners of the bathhouses that was raided, which was one of the major talking points of BLM.  He said he did not support the police being banned at all, even though he was there as the police smashed the bathhouse to pieces in front of his eyes.\n\nHe felt there had been so much work put into the relationship with the police over the years, that the ban was going to set us back in all of that and he didn’t want to see it all be for nothing.  So he and I stood in front of the pride office door one early January morning, holding a sign that said Not my Pride.\n\nWith that, things evolved into a petition of over 10,000 signatures which John Tory ignored, and the attempt to create a space where police were welcome, in or out of uniform, complete with performers from the community including several who are transgender and people of colour, who each volunteered their time to perform and show support for the police remaining in the parade.\n\nI took a lot of heat for organizing this event, there were lots of opinions about it, but my motivation was simply to say I didn’t support setting our community back like this and I am just as much allowed to try to keep things from falling apart as BLM was in trying to deconstruct them.\n\nDon’t get me wrong.  It is easy to assume that by doing this, I didn’t think there was any issue with the police and their treatment of people of colour or minorities in this city.  And it is easy to assume that I didn’t support the goal of BLM with regards to addressing racism and systemic inequalities that are not faced by white and white-passing people (such as myself, even as an Indigenous person, that looks white).  But both are entirely untrue.\n\nIn fact, if the tactics of BLM were not so clearly divisive and held even some iota of a way of uniting people and a narrative that made sense, unformed police triggering a ban necessity not remotely meeting the level of common sense that the vast majority of the city and even many people of colour, felt was necessary to take such drastic steps, then they probably would have had my support.\n\nI wrote to BLM and requested and offered to meet with them, their response was to attack me on Twitter.  I filed a human rights complaint and they attacked that.  It’s fine, there was a certain amount of optics to the entire situation that required arguments to be spelled out clearly since BLM’s script kept changing whenever common sense poked through and their actions weren’t aligning with what they were saying.\n\nBut I think with better leadership they actually could have accomplished quite a bit.  As I said at the time, and I reiterate today, I hope I don’t know anyone who thinks that there isn’t a racism problem because we should all know there is one.  And a lot more work needs to be done.  It will always need to be done because so long as there are hierarchal power structures, there will exist attempts to silence and minimize marginalized groups first. \n\nAnd I have changed my views on a few things in fact, so I wouldn’t say that their efforts were entirely for naught.\n\nOne example was the proposal that a social worker should be in police cars so that we have a professional ready to diffuse situations where police are being put into situations of applying force when it may not be necessary.  At the time I felt it was financially not feasible but I’ve come to realize that it is both feasible but also a very smart idea.  And I believe it would work.  I didn’t at the time.\n\nCould I have been convinced without all the drama?  Probably. I think most people are reasonable enough to consider new ideas when they are presented properly and in this case even when they are presented improperly if the person is open to it.\n\nThe problem is that I forced myself to remain open because I was in a battle that necessitated being sure of my positions or relatively sure and ready to adapt if necessary.  But most of the public neither has the time or interest to do that so if they aren’t given a clear and compelling argument quickly, the message gets lost.\n\nI changed my views on some things.  I’m not sure how many other Torontonians did so though, Pride almost went bankrupt after these dramatic events as supporters, individual and corporate, pulled funding and money in opposition to the decision to ban the police.\n\nI was also looking at some of the cases in the US where people of colour were being savagely beaten by police and the reactions of organizations of BLM, which in fairness really ought know no limits in terms of a response.  If people who aren’t people of colour were living in a community that was being routinely and systemically being treated like this, the response should be and must be harsh and swift.\n\nBut the response can’t turn into financial theft, nor calling our Prime Minister a white supremacist terrorist, when at that same time Donald Trump was president and I think most people would agree he is at least one of those two things, if not both.  That kind of rhetoric only stood to hurt the message, which is probably why the leader who said it was removed shortly after.  But the requirement to ban uniformed police officers was also an incoherent and nonsensical narrative that to this day doesn’t make sense even to many of the people who once yelled it from the roof tops.\n\nThe limitations that opposition and protestors should face should come from within, from organized leadership that is prepared to deliver a message that people can buy into and that unites people behind a common purpose with clear and logical calls to action.  BLM lacked that but had garnered the power to create chaos, something its leaders clearly acknowledged was part of the plan, but without one as to what to do with that power.  Creating chaos does little of all that’s left when the excitement an","startDate":"2026-05-08T10:00:00-04:00","endDate":"2026-05-08T11:00:00-04:00","isAllDay":false,"htmlLink":"https://www.google.com/calendar/event?eid=Xzg0cTQyaDlpOGQwamNiOW42c3NrY2I5azhvcGpnYjlvNjBvM2diYTY3NG9qY2dwZzZncmpjZDlvOGNfMjAyNjA1MDhUMTQwMDAwWiBicnluLmhlbmRyaWNrc0Bt","source":"google"},{"id":"_8l1k8dhk8l2j4b9h60q38b9k6l334ba1890jib9k8cp36d2584p44ci484_20260508T150000Z","title":"Should have 10000 Facebook followers according to projections on August 18","description":"","startDate":"2026-05-08T11:00:00-04:00","endDate":"2026-05-08T12:00:00-04:00","isAllDay":false,"htmlLink":"https://www.google.com/calendar/event?eid=XzhsMWs4ZGhrOGwyajRiOWg2MHEzOGI5azZsMzM0YmExODkwamliOWs4Y3AzNmQyNTg0cDQ0Y2k0ODRfMjAyNjA1MDhUMTUwMDAwWiBicnluLmhlbmRyaWNrc0Bt","source":"google"},{"id":"_6ooj4dpp84qk2ba58orj8b9k74q4cb9o6ko3ab9k8l246d1j6p2j8h9h6s_20260508T160000Z","title":"Request meeting from Viola regarding course availability and potential replacement courses ","description":"","startDate":"2026-05-08T12:00:00-04:00","endDate":"2026-05-08T13:00:00-04:00","isAllDay":false,"htmlLink":"https://www.google.com/calendar/event?eid=XzZvb2o0ZHBwODRxazJiYTU4b3JqOGI5azc0cTRjYjlvNmtvM2FiOWs4bDI0NmQxajZwMmo4aDloNnNfMjAyNjA1MDhUMTYwMDAwWiBicnluLmhlbmRyaWNrc0Bt","source":"google"},{"id":"_6l24cd1o60rjib9o6sojgb9k8l1k2ba16sqk6b9n6kqjidpj88q30e1k6o_20260508T210000Z","title":"Bring rollerblades for Cherry blossoms","description":"","startDate":"2026-05-08T17:00:00-04:00","endDate":"2026-05-08T18:00:00-04:00","isAllDay":false,"htmlLink":"https://www.google.com/calendar/event?eid=XzZsMjRjZDFvNjByamliOW82c29qZ2I5azhsMWsyYmExNnNxazZiOW42a3FqaWRwajg4cTMwZTFrNm9fMjAyNjA1MDhUMjEwMDAwWiBicnluLmhlbmRyaWNrc0Bt","source":"google"},{"id":"_84q42h9i8d0jcb9n6sskcb9k8opjgb9o60o3gba674ojcgpg6grjcd9o8c_20260509T140000Z","title":"Prepare for 10 year anniversary of blm parade block - see notes","description":"Almost 10 years ago, Black Lives Matter held a sit in, demanding then Executive Director, @mathieu chantelois, agree to ban uniformed police officers from marching in the Pride Parade.\n\nSubsequently, when the Pride organization voted to support the ban, it created deep divisions in the city, and throughout the 2LGBTQ+ community in particular.\n\nAt the time, I took a very public stance.  My view was that there has already been so much taken away from our community, that creating deeper divisions without any clear narrative that would solve anything, was not going to be helpful for the community as a whole and likely was not even going to be helpful in addressing issues of racism.\n\nThe leaders of BLM in Toronto had just faced serious financial impropriety allegations, the primary leader had just went on a microphone publicly calling Justin Trudeau a white supremacist terrorist, amid a litany of other mayhem that might have seemed like great activism to those serving it, but to the average person not only came across as disorganized and at times violent, but also lacked any coherent talking points other than people of colour felt triggered by seeing police in uniform marching in the parade, while they stood a few feet away in full uniform on the sidelines providing city-funded protection to parade goers.\n\nAs I watched the divisions deepening by the hour, with the left launching its usual well-orchestrated politically correct rhetoric, the right firming its stance that nothing was wrong and there was nothing to see or do here except let the police back in, I looked at the situation and realized what ultimately ended up happening, which was that without a clear narrative that made sense, and without some sort of a plan to clean up the divisions, all that was happening was a division that not only fractured out community but also put us front and center to the rest of the city as a community that didn’t know what it wanted, didn’t even know what it wanted to say, and we were willing to go to extremes to be heard about it.  I’ve lost a lot in the process of being outted and coming out, I’ve also lost a lot because of the Church, and I’ve spent a long time learning about myself and learning about my community, enough to know that what Pride was doing, without much transparency, was wrong and it did not represent me.\n\nI met with one of the owners of the bathhouses that was raided, which was one of the major talking points of BLM.  He said he did not support the police being banned at all, even though he was there as the police smashed the bathhouse to pieces in front of his eyes.\n\nHe felt there had been so much work put into the relationship with the police over the years, that the ban was going to set us back in all of that and he didn’t want to see it all be for nothing.  So he and I stood in front of the pride office door one early January morning, holding a sign that said Not my Pride.\n\nWith that, things evolved into a petition of over 10,000 signatures which John Tory ignored, and the attempt to create a space where police were welcome, in or out of uniform, complete with performers from the community including several who are transgender and people of colour, who each volunteered their time to perform and show support for the police remaining in the parade.\n\nI took a lot of heat for organizing this event, there were lots of opinions about it, but my motivation was simply to say I didn’t support setting our community back like this and I am just as much allowed to try to keep things from falling apart as BLM was in trying to deconstruct them.\n\nDon’t get me wrong.  It is easy to assume that by doing this, I didn’t think there was any issue with the police and their treatment of people of colour or minorities in this city.  And it is easy to assume that I didn’t support the goal of BLM with regards to addressing racism and systemic inequalities that are not faced by white and white-passing people (such as myself, even as an Indigenous person, that looks white).  But both are entirely untrue.\n\nIn fact, if the tactics of BLM were not so clearly divisive and held even some iota of a way of uniting people and a narrative that made sense, unformed police triggering a ban necessity not remotely meeting the level of common sense that the vast majority of the city and even many people of colour, felt was necessary to take such drastic steps, then they probably would have had my support.\n\nI wrote to BLM and requested and offered to meet with them, their response was to attack me on Twitter.  I filed a human rights complaint and they attacked that.  It’s fine, there was a certain amount of optics to the entire situation that required arguments to be spelled out clearly since BLM’s script kept changing whenever common sense poked through and their actions weren’t aligning with what they were saying.\n\nBut I think with better leadership they actually could have accomplished quite a bit.  As I said at the time, and I reiterate today, I hope I don’t know anyone who thinks that there isn’t a racism problem because we should all know there is one.  And a lot more work needs to be done.  It will always need to be done because so long as there are hierarchal power structures, there will exist attempts to silence and minimize marginalized groups first. \n\nAnd I have changed my views on a few things in fact, so I wouldn’t say that their efforts were entirely for naught.\n\nOne example was the proposal that a social worker should be in police cars so that we have a professional ready to diffuse situations where police are being put into situations of applying force when it may not be necessary.  At the time I felt it was financially not feasible but I’ve come to realize that it is both feasible but also a very smart idea.  And I believe it would work.  I didn’t at the time.\n\nCould I have been convinced without all the drama?  Probably. I think most people are reasonable enough to consider new ideas when they are presented properly and in this case even when they are presented improperly if the person is open to it.\n\nThe problem is that I forced myself to remain open because I was in a battle that necessitated being sure of my positions or relatively sure and ready to adapt if necessary.  But most of the public neither has the time or interest to do that so if they aren’t given a clear and compelling argument quickly, the message gets lost.\n\nI changed my views on some things.  I’m not sure how many other Torontonians did so though, Pride almost went bankrupt after these dramatic events as supporters, individual and corporate, pulled funding and money in opposition to the decision to ban the police.\n\nI was also looking at some of the cases in the US where people of colour were being savagely beaten by police and the reactions of organizations of BLM, which in fairness really ought know no limits in terms of a response.  If people who aren’t people of colour were living in a community that was being routinely and systemically being treated like this, the response should be and must be harsh and swift.\n\nBut the response can’t turn into financial theft, nor calling our Prime Minister a white supremacist terrorist, when at that same time Donald Trump was president and I think most people would agree he is at least one of those two things, if not both. It is easy to assume that by doing this, I didn’t think there was any issue with the police and their treatment of people of colour or minorities in this city.  And it is easy to assume that I didn’t support the goal of BLM with regards to addressing racism and systemic inequalities that are not faced by white and white-passing people (such as myself, even as an Indigenous person, that looks white).  But both are entirely untrue.\n\nIn fact, if the tactics of BLM were not so clearly divisive and held even some iota of a way of uniting people and a narrative that made sense, unformed police triggering a ban necessity not remotely meeting the level of common sense that the vast majority of the city and even many people of colour, felt was necessary to take such drastic steps, then they probably would have had my support.\n\nI wrote to BLM and requested and offered to meet with them, their response was to attack me on Twitter.  I filed a human rights complaint and they attacked that.  It’s fine, there was a certain amount of optics to the entire situation that required arguments to be spelled out clearly since BLM’s script kept changing whenever common sense poked through and their actions weren’t aligning with what they were saying.\n\nBut I think with better leadership they actually could have accomplished quite a bit.  As I said at the time, and I reiterate today, I hope I don’t know anyone who thinks that there isn’t a racism problem because we should all know there is one.  And a lot more work needs to be done.  It will always need to be done because so long as there are hierarchal power structures, there will exist attempts to silence and minimize marginalized groups first. \n\nAnd I have changed my views on a few things in fact, so I wouldn’t say that their efforts were entirely for naught.\n\nOne example was the proposal that a social worker should be in police cars so that we have a professional ready to diffuse situations where police are being put into situations of applying force when it may not be necessary.  At the time I felt it was financially not feasible but I’ve come to realize that it is both feasible but also a very smart idea.  And I believe it would work.  I didn’t at the time.\n\nCould I have been convinced without all the drama?  Probably. I think most people are reasonable enough to consider new ideas when they are presented properly and in this case even when they are presented improperly if the person is open to it.\n\nThe problem is that I forced myself to remain open because I was in a battle that necessitated being sure of my positions or relatively sure and ready to adapt if necessary.  But most of the public neither has the time or interest to do that so if they aren’t given a clear and compelling argument quickly, the message gets lost.\n\nI changed my views on some things.  I’m not sure how many other Torontonians did so though, Pride almost went bankrupt after these dramatic events as supporters, individual and corporate, pulled funding and money in opposition to the decision to ban the police.\n\nI was also looking at some of the cases in the US where people of colour were being savagely beaten by police and the reactions of organizations of BLM, which in fairness really ought know no limits in terms of a response.  If people who aren’t people of colour were living in a community that was being routinely and systemically being treated like this, the response should be and must be harsh and swift.\n\nBut the response can’t turn into financial theft, nor calling our Prime Minister a white supremacist terrorist, when at that same time Donald Trump was president and I think most people would agree he is at least one of those two things, if not both.  That kind of rhetoric only stood to hurt the message, which is probably why the leader who said it was removed shortly after.  But the requirement to ban uniformed police officers was also an incoherent and nonsensical narrative that to this day doesn’t make sense even to many of the people who once yelled it from the roof tops.\n\nThe limitations that opposition and protestors should face should come from within, from organized leadership that is prepared to deliver a message that people can buy into and that unites people behind a common purpose with clear and logical calls to action.  BLM lacked that but had garnered the power to create chaos, something its leaders clearly acknowledged was part of the plan, but without one as to what to do with that power. for 10 year anniversary of blm parade block - see notes","description":"Almost 10 years ago, Black Lives Matter held a sit in, demanding then Executive Director, @mathieu chantelois, agree to ban uniformed police officers from marching in the Pride Parade.\n\nSubsequently, when the Pride organization voted to support the ban, it created deep divisions in the city, and throughout the 2LGBTQ+ community in particular.\n\nAt the time, I took a very public stance.  My view was that there has already been so much taken away from our community, that creating deeper divisions without any clear narrative that would solve anything, was not going to be helpful for the community as a whole and likely was not even going to be helpful in addressing issues of racism.\n\nThe leaders of BLM in Toronto had just faced serious financial impropriety allegations, the primary leader had just went on a microphone publicly calling Justin Trudeau a white supremacist terrorist, amid a litany of other mayhem that might have seemed like great activism to those serving it, but to the average person not only came across as disorganized and at times violent, but also lacked any coherent talking points other than people of colour felt triggered by seeing police in uniform marching in the parade, while they stood a few feet away in full uniform on the sidelines providing city-funded protection to parade goers.\n\nAs I watched the divisions deepening by the hour, with the left launching its usual well-orchestrated politically correct rhetoric, the right firming its stance that nothing was wrong and there was nothing to see or do here except let the police back in, I looked at the situation and realized what ultimately ended up happening, which was that without a clear narrative that made sense, and without some sort of a plan to clean up the divisions, all that was happening was a division that not only fractured out community but also put us front and center to the rest of the city as a community that didn’t know what it wanted, didn’t even know what it wanted to say, and we were willing to go to extremes to be heard about it.  I’ve lost a lot in the process of being outted and coming out, I’ve also lost a lot because of the Church, and I’ve spent a long time learning about myself and learning about my community, enough to know that what Pride was doing, without much transparency, was wrong and it did not represent me.\n\nI met with one of the owners of the bathhouses that was raided, which was one of the major talking points of BLM.  He said he did not support the police being banned at all, even though he was there as the police smashed the bathhouse to pieces in front of his eyes.\n\nHe felt there had been so much work put into the relationship with the police over the years, that the ban was going to set us back in all of that and he didn’t want to see it all be for nothing.  So he and I stood in front of the pride office door one early January morning, holding a sign that said Not my Pride.\n\nWith that, things evolved into a petition of over 10,000 signatures which John Tory ignored, and the attempt to create a space where police were welcome, in or out of uniform, complete with performers from the community including several who are transgender and people of colour, who each volunteered their time to perform and show support for the police remaining in the parade.\n\nI took a lot of heat for organizing this event, there were lots of opinions about it, but my motivation was simply to say I didn’t support setting our community back like this and I am just as much allowed to try to keep things from falling apart as BLM was in trying to deconstruct them.\n\nDon’t get me wrong.  It is easy to assume that by doing this, I didn’t think there was any issue with the police and their treatment of people of colour or minorities in this city.  And it is easy to assume that I didn’t support the goal of BLM with regards to addressing racism and systemic inequalities that are not faced by white and white-passing people (such as myself, even as an Indigenous person, that looks white).  But both are entirely untrue.\n\nIn fact, if the tactics of BLM were not so clearly divisive and held even some iota of a way of uniting people and a narrative that made sense, unformed police triggering a ban necessity not remotely meeting the level of common sense that the vast majority of the city and even many people of colour, felt was necessary to take such drastic steps, then they probably would have had my support.\n\nI wrote to BLM and requested and offered to meet with them, their response was to attack me on Twitter.  I filed a human rights complaint and they attacked that.  It’s fine, there was a certain amount of optics to the entire situation that required arguments to be spelled out clearly since BLM’s script kept changing whenever common sense poked through and their actions weren’t aligning with what they were saying.\n\nBut I think with better leadership they actually could have accomplished quite a bit.  As I said at the time, and I reiterate today, I hope I don’t know anyone who thinks that there isn’t a racism problem because we should all know there is one.  And a lot more work needs to be done.  It will always need to be done because so long as there are hierarchal power structures, there will exist attempts to silence and minimize marginalized groups first. \n\nAnd I have changed my views on a few things in fact, so I wouldn’t say that their efforts were entirely for naught.\n\nOne example was the proposal that a social worker should be in police cars so that we have a professional ready to diffuse situations where police are being put into situations of applying force when it may not be necessary.  At the time I felt it was financially not feasible but I’ve come to realize that it is both feasible but also a very smart idea.  And I believe it would work.  I didn’t at the time.\n\nCould I have been convinced without all the drama?  Probably. I think most people are reasonable enough to consider new ideas when they are presented properly and in this case even when they are presented improperly if the person is open to it.\n\nThe problem is that I forced myself to remain open because I was in a battle that necessitated being sure of my positions or relatively sure and ready to adapt if necessary.  But most of the public neither has the time or interest to do that so if they aren’t given a clear and compelling argument quickly, the message gets lost.\n\nI changed my views on some things.  I’m not sure how many other Torontonians did so though, Pride almost went bankrupt after these dramatic events as supporters, individual and corporate, pulled funding and money in opposition to the decision to ban the police.\n\nI was also looking at some of the cases in the US where people of colour were being savagely beaten by police and the reactions of organizations of BLM, which in fairness really ought know no limits in terms of a response.  If people who aren’t people of colour were living in a community that was being routinely and systemically being treated like this, the response should be and must be harsh and swift.\n\nBut the response can’t turn into financial theft, nor calling our Prime Minister a white supremacist terrorist, when at that same time Donald Trump was president and I think most people would agree he is at least one of those two things, if not both. Philosophy of Religion I","description":"","startDate":"2026-05-12T13:00:00-04:00","endDate":"2026-05-12T16:00:00-04:00","isAllDay":false,"htmlLink":"https://www.google.com/calendar/event?eid=MnY4bXVjczU5NzhmYTlxaGtwNG5yMTlxaGdfMjAyNjA1MTJUMTcwMDAwWiBicnluLmhlbmRyaWNrc0Bt","source":"google"},{"id":"_6l24cd1o60rjib9o6sojgb9k8l1k2ba16sqk6b9n6kqjidpj88q30e1k6o_20260512T210000Z","title":"Bring rollerblades for Cherry blossoms","description":"","startDate":"2026-05-12T17:00:00-04:00","endDate":"2026-05-12T18:00:00-04:00","isAllDay":false,"htmlLink":"https://www.google.com/calendar/event?eid=XzZsMjRjZDFvNjByamliOW82c29qZ2I5azhsMWsyYmExNnNxazZiOW42a3FqaWRwajg4cTMwZTFrNm9fMjAyNjA1MTJUMjEwMDAwWiBicnluLmhlbmRyaWNrc0Bt","source":"google"},{"id":"_84q42h9i8d0jcb9n6sskcb9k8opjgb9o60o3gba674ojcgpg6grjcd9o8c_20260513T140000Z","title":"Prepare for 10 year anniversary of blm parade block - see notes","description":"Almost 10 years ago, Black Lives Matter held a sit in, demanding then Executive Director, @mathieu chantelois, agree to ban uniformed police officers from marching in the Pride Parade.\n\nSubsequently, when the Pride organization voted to support the ban, it created deep divisions in the city, and throughout the 2LGBTQ+ community in particular.\n\nAt the time, I took a very public stance.  My view was that there has already been so much taken away from our community, that creating deeper divisions without any clear narrative that would solve anything, was not going to be helpful for the community as a whole and likely was not even going to be helpful in addressing issues of racism.\n\nThe leaders of BLM in Toronto had just faced serious financial impropriety allegations, the primary leader had just went on a microphone publicly calling Justin Trudeau a white supremacist terrorist, amid a litany of other mayhem that might have seemed like great activism to those serving it, but to the average person not only came across as disorganized and at times violent, but also lacked any coherent talking points other than people of colour felt triggered by seeing police in uniform marching in the parade, while they stood a few feet away in full uniform on the sidelines providing city-funded protection to parade goers.\n\nAs I watched the divisions deepening by the hour, with the left launching its usual well-orchestrated politically correct rhetoric, the right firming its stance that nothing was wrong and there was nothing to see or do here except let the police back in, I looked at the situation and realized what ultimately ended up happening, which was that without a clear narrative that made sense, and without some sort of a plan to clean up the divisions, all that was happening was a division that not only fractured out community but also put us front and center to the rest of the city as a community that didn’t know what it wanted, didn’t even know what it wanted to say, and we were willing to go to extremes to be heard about it.  I’ve lost a lot in the process of being outted and coming out, I’ve also lost a lot because of the Church, and I’ve spent a long time learning about myself and learning about my community, enough to know that what Pride was doing, without much transparency, was wrong and it did not represent me.\n\nI met with one of the owners of the bathhouses that was raided, which was one of the major talking points of BLM.  He said he did not support the police being banned at all, even though he was there as the police smashed the bathhouse to pieces in front of his eyes.\n\nHe felt there had been so much work put into the relationship with the police over the years, that the ban was going to set us back in all of that and he didn’t want to see it all be for nothing.  So he and I stood in front of the pride office door one early January morning, holding a sign that said Not my Pride.\n\nWith that, things evolved into a petition of over 10,000 signatures which John Tory ignored, and the attempt to create a space where police were welcome, in or out of uniform, complete with performers from the community including several who are transgender and people of colour, who each volunteered their time to perform and show support for the police remaining in the parade.\n\nI took a lot of heat for organizing this event, there were lots of opinions about it, but my motivation was simply to say I didn’t support setting our community back like this and I am just as much allowed to try to keep things from falling apart as BLM was in trying to deconstruct them.\n\nDon’t get me wrong.  It is easy to assume that by doing this, I didn’t think there was any issue with the police and their treatment of people of colour or minorities in this city.  And it is easy to assume that I didn’t support the goal of BLM with regards to addressing racism and systemic inequalities that are not faced by white and white-passing people (such as myself, even as an Indigenous person, that looks white).  But both are entirely untrue.\n\nIn fact, if the tactics of BLM were not so clearly divisive and held even some iota of a way of uniting people and a narrative that made sense, unformed police triggering a ban necessity not remotely meeting the level of common sense that the vast majority of the city and even many people of colour, felt was necessary to take such drastic steps, then they probably would have had my support.\n\nI wrote to BLM and requested and offered to meet with them, their response was to attack me on Twitter.  I filed a human rights complaint and they attacked that.  It’s fine, there was a certain amount of optics to the entire situation that required arguments to be spelled out clearly since BLM’s script kept changing whenever common sense poked through and their actions weren’t aligning with what they were saying.\n\nBut I think with better leadership they actually could have accomplished quite a bit.  As I said at the time, and I reiterate today, I hope I don’t know anyone who thinks that there isn’t a racism problem because we should all know there is one.  And a lot more work needs to be done.  It will always need to be done because so long as there are hierarchal power structures, there will exist attempts to silence and minimize marginalized groups first. \n\nAnd I have changed my views on a few things in fact, so I wouldn’t say that their efforts were entirely for naught.\n\nOne example was the proposal that a social worker should be in police cars so that we have a professional ready to diffuse situations where police are being put into situations of applying force when it may not be necessary.  At the time I felt it was financially not feasible but I’ve come to realize that it is both feasible but also a very smart idea.  And I believe it would work.  I didn’t at the time.\n\nCould I have been convinced without all the drama?  Probably. I think most people are reasonable enough to consider new ideas when they are presented properly and in this case even when they are presented improperly if the person is open to it.\n\nThe problem is that I forced myself to remain open because I was in a battle that necessitated being sure of my positions or relatively sure and ready to adapt if necessary.  But most of the public neither has the time or interest to do that so if they aren’t given a clear and compelling argument quickly, the message gets lost.\n\nI changed my views on some things.  I’m not sure how many other Torontonians did so though, Pride almost went bankrupt after these dramatic events as supporters, individual and corporate, pulled funding and money in opposition to the decision to ban the police.\n\nI was also looking at some of the cases in the US where people of colour were being savagely beaten by police and the reactions of organizations of BLM, which in fairness really ought know no limits in terms of a response.  If people who aren’t people of colour were living in a community that was being routinely and systemically being treated like this, the response should be and must be harsh and swift.\n\nBut the response can’t turn into financial theft, nor calling our Prime Minister a white supremacist terrorist, when at that same time Donald Trump was president and I think most people would agree he is at least one of those two things, if not both.  That kind of rhetoric only stood to hurt the message, which is probably why the leader who said it was removed shortly after.  But the requirement to ban uniformed police officers was also an incoherent and nonsensical narrative that to this day doesn’t make sense even to many of the people who once yelled it from the roof tops.\n\nThe limitations that opposition and protestors should face should come from within, from organized leadership that is prepared to deliver a message that people can buy into and that unites people behind a common purpose with clear and logical calls to action.  BLM lacked that but had garnered the power to create chaos, something its leaders clearly acknowledged was part of the plan, but without one as to what to do with that power.  Creating chaos does little of all that’s left when the excitement an","startDate":"2026-05-13T10:00:00-04:00","endDate":"2026-05-13T11:00:00-04:00","isAllDay":false,"htmlLink":"https://www.google.com/calendar/event?eid=Xzg0cTQyaDlpOGQwamNiOW42c3NrY2I5azhvcGpnYjlvNjBvM2diYTY3NG9qY2dwZzZncmpjZDlvOGNfMjAyNjA1MTNUMTQwMDAwWiBicnluLmhlbmRyaWNrc0Bt","source":"google"},{"id":"_8l1k8dhk8l2j4b9h60q38b9k6l334ba1890jib9k8cp36d2584p44ci484_20260513T150000Z","title":"Should have 10000 Facebook followers according to projections on August 18","description":"","startDate":"2026-05-13T11:00:00-04:00","endDate":"2026-05-13T12:00:00-04:00","isAllDay":false,"htmlLink":"https://www.google.com/calendar/event?eid=XzhsMWs4ZGhrOGwyajRiOWg2MHEzOGI5azZsMzM0YmExODkwamliOWs4Y3AzNmQyNTg0cDQ0Y2k0ODRfMjAyNjA1MTNUMTUwMDAwWiBicnluLmhlbmRyaWNrc0Bt","source":"google"},{"id":"_6ooj4dpp84qk2ba58orj8b9k74q4cb9o6ko3ab9k8l246d1j6p2j8h9h6s_20260513T160000Z","title":"Request meeting from Viola regarding course availability and potential replacement courses ","description":"","startDate":"2026-05-13T12:00:00-04:00","endDate":"2026-05-13T13:00:00-04:00","isAllDay":false,"htmlLink":"https://www.google.com/calendar/event?eid=XzZvb2o0ZHBwODRxazJiYTU4b3JqOGI5azc0cTRjYjlvNmtvM2FiOWs4bDI0NmQxajZwMmo4aDloNnNfMjAyNjA1MTNUMTYwMDAwWiBicnluLmhlbmRyaWNrc0Bt","source":"google"},{"id":"_6l24cd1o60rjib9o6sojgb9k8l1k2ba16sqk6b9n6kqjidpj88q30e1k6o_20260513T210000Z","title":"Bring rollerblades for Cherry blossoms","description":"","startDate":"2026-05-13T17:00:00-04:00","endDate":"2026-05-13T18:00:00-04:00","isAllDay":false,"htmlLink":"https://www.google.com/calendar/event?eid=XzZsMjRjZDFvNjByamliOW82c29qZ2I5azhsMWsyYmExNnNxazZiOW42a3FqaWRwajg4cTMwZTFrNm9fMjAyNjA1MTNUMjEwMDAwWiBicnluLmhlbmRyaWNrc0Bt","source":"google"},{"id":"_84q42h9i8d0jcb9n6sskcb9k8opjgb9o60o3gba674ojcgpg6grjcd9o8c_20260514T140000Z","title":"Prepare for 10 year anniversary of blm parade block - But both are entirely untrue.\n\nIn fact, if the tactics of BLM were not so clearly divisive and held even some iota of a way of uniting people and a narrative that made sense, unformed police triggering a ban necessity not remotely meeting the level of common sense that the vast majority of the city and even many people of colour, felt was necessary to take such drastic steps, then they probably would have had my support.\n\nI wrote to BLM and requested and offered to meet with them, their response was to attack me on Twitter.  I filed a human rights complaint and they attacked that.  It’s fine, there was a certain amount of optics to the entire situation that required arguments to be spelled out clearly since BLM’s script kept changing whenever common sense poked through and their actions weren’t aligning with what they were saying.\n\nBut I think with better leadership they actually could have accomplished quite a bit.  As I said at the time, and I reiterate today, I hope I don’t know anyone who thinks that there isn’t a racism problem because we should all know there is one.  And a lot more work needs to be done.  It will always need to be done because so long as there are hierarchal power structures, there will exist attempts to silence and minimize marginalized groups first. \n\nAnd I have changed my views on a few things in fact, so I wouldn’t say that their efforts were entirely for naught.\n\nOne example was the proposal that a social worker should be in police cars so that we have a professional ready to diffuse situations where police are being put into situations of applying force when it may not be necessary.  At the time I felt it was financially not feasible but I’ve come to realize that it is both feasible but also a very smart idea.  And I believe it would work.  I didn’t at the time.\n\nCould I have been convinced without all the drama?  Probably. I think most people are reasonable enough to consider new ideas when they are presented properly and in this case even when they are presented improperly if the person is open to it.\n\nThe problem is that I forced myself to remain open because I was in a battle that necessitated being sure of my positions or relatively sure and ready to adapt if necessary.  But most of the public neither has the time or interest to do that so if they aren’t given a clear and compelling argument quickly, the message gets lost.\n\nI changed my views on some things.  I’m not sure how many other Torontonians did so though, Pride almost went bankrupt after these dramatic events as supporters, individual and corporate, pulled funding and money in opposition to the decision to ban the police.\n\nI was also looking at some of the cases in the US where people of colour were being savagely beaten by police and the reactions of organizations of BLM, which in fairness really ought know no limits in terms of a response.  If people who aren’t people of colour were living in a community that was being routinely and systemically being treated like this, the response should be and must be harsh and swift.\n\nBut the response can’t turn into financial theft, nor calling our Prime Minister a white supremacist terrorist, when at that same time Donald Trump was president and I think most people would agree he is at least one of those two things, if not both.  That kind of rhetoric only stood to hurt the message, which is probably why the leader who said it was removed shortly after.  But the requirement to ban uniformed police officers was also an incoherent and nonsensical narrative that to this day doesn’t make sense even to many of the people who once yelled it from the roof tops.\n\nThe limitations that opposition and protestors should face should come from within, from organized leadership that is prepared to deliver a message that people can buy into and that unites people behind a common purpose with clear and logical calls to action.  BLM lacked that but had garnered the power to create chaos, something its leaders clearly acknowledged was part of the plan, but without one as to what to do with that power.  Creating chaos does little of all that’s left when the excitement for 10 year anniversary of blm parade block - see notes","description":"Almost 10 years ago, Black Lives Matter held a sit in, demanding then Executive Director, @mathieu chantelois, agree to ban uniformed police officers from marching in the Pride Parade.\n\nSubsequently, when the Pride organization voted to support the ban, it created deep divisions in the city, and throughout the 2LGBTQ+ community in particular.\n\nAt the time, I took a very public stance.  My view was that there has already been so much taken away from our community, that creating deeper divisions without any clear narrative that would solve anything, was not going to be helpful for the community as a whole and likely was not even going to be helpful in addressing issues of racism.\n\nThe leaders of BLM in Toronto had just faced serious financial impropriety allegations, the primary leader had just went on a microphone publicly calling Justin Trudeau a white supremacist terrorist, amid a litany of other mayhem that might have seemed like great activism to those serving it, but to the average person not only came across as disorganized and at times violent, but also lacked any coherent talking points other than people of colour felt triggered by seeing police in uniform marching in the parade, while they stood a few feet away in full uniform on the sidelines providing city-funded protection to parade goers.\n\nAs I watched the divisions deepening by the hour, with the left launching its usual well-orchestrated politically correct rhetoric, the right firming its stance that nothing was wrong and there was nothing to see or do here except let the police back in, I looked at the situation and realized what ultimately ended up happening, which was that without a clear narrative that made sense, and without some sort of a plan to clean up the divisions, all that was happening was a division that not only fractured out community but also put us front and center to the rest of the city as a community that didn’t know what it wanted, didn’t even know what it wanted to say, and we were willing to go to extremes to be heard about it.  I’ve lost a lot in the process of being outted and coming out, I’ve also lost a lot because of the Church, and I’ve spent a long time learning about myself and learning about my community, enough to know that what Pride was doing, without much transparency, was wrong and it did not represent me.\n\nI met with one of the owners of the bathhouses that was raided, which was one of the major talking points of BLM.  He said he did not support the police being banned at all, even though he was there as the police smashed the bathhouse to pieces in front of his eyes.\n\nHe felt there had been so much work put into the relationship with the police over the years, that the ban was going to set us back in all of that and he didn’t want to see it all be for nothing.  So he and I stood in front of the pride office door one early January morning, holding a sign that said Not my Pride.\n\nWith that, things evolved into a petition of over 10,000 signatures which John Tory ignored, and the attempt to create a space where police were welcome, in or out of uniform, complete with performers from the community including several who are transgender and people of colour, who each volunteered their time to perform and show support for the police remaining in the parade.\n\nI took a lot of heat for organizing this event, there were lots of opinions about it, but my motivation was simply to say I didn’t support setting our community back like this and I am just as much allowed to try to keep things from falling apart as BLM was in trying to deconstruct them.\n\nDon’t get me wrong.  It is easy to assume that by doing this, I didn’t think there was any issue with the police and their treatment of people of colour or minorities in this city.  And it is easy to assume that I didn’t support the goal of BLM with regards to addressing racism and systemic inequalities that are not faced by white and white-passing people (such as myself, even as an Indigenous person, that looks white).  But both are entirely untrue.\n\nIn fact, if the tactics of BLM were not so clearly divisive and held even some iota of a way of uniting people and a narrative that made sense, unformed police triggering a ban necessity not remotely meeting the level of common sense that the vast majority of the city and even many people of colour, felt was necessary to take such drastic steps, then they probably would have had my support.\n\nI wrote to BLM and requested and offered to meet with them, their response was to attack me on Twitter.  I filed a human rights complaint and they attacked that.  It’s fine, there was a certain amount of optics to the entire situation that required arguments to be spelled out clearly since BLM’s script kept changing whenever common sense poked through and their actions weren’t aligning with what they were saying.\n\nBut I think with better leadership they actually could have accomplished quite a bit.  As I said at the time, and I reiterate today, I hope I don’t know anyone who thinks that there isn’t a racism problem because we should all know there is one.  And a lot more work needs to be done.  It will always need to be done because so long as there are hierarchal power structures, there will exist attempts to silence and minimize marginalized groups first. \n\nAnd I have changed my views on a few things in fact, so I wouldn’t say that their efforts were entirely for naught.\n\nOne example was the proposal that a social worker should be in police cars so that we have a professional ready to diffuse situations where police are being put into situations of applying force when it may not be necessary.  At the time I felt it was financially not feasible but I’ve come to realize that it is both feasible but also a very smart idea.  And I believe it would work.  I didn’t at the time.\n\nCould I have been convinced without all the drama?  Probably. I think most people are reasonable enough to consider new ideas when they are presented properly and in this case even when they are presented improperly if the person is open to it.\n\nThe problem is that I forced myself to remain open because I was in a battle that necessitated being sure of my positions or relatively sure and ready to adapt if necessary.  But most of the public neither has the time or interest to do that so if they aren’t given a clear and compelling argument quickly, the message gets lost.\n\nI changed my views on some things.  I’m not sure how many other Torontonians did so though, Pride almost went bankrupt after these dramatic events as supporters, individual and corporate, pulled funding and money in opposition to the decision to ban the police.\n\nI was also looking at some of the cases in the US where people of colour were being savagely beaten by police and the reactions of organizations of BLM, which in fairness really ought know no limits in terms of a response.  If people who aren’t people of colour were living in a community that was being routinely and systemically being treated like this, the response should be and must be harsh and swift.\n\nBut the response can’t turn into financial theft, nor calling our Prime Minister a white supremacist terrorist, when at that same time Donald Trump was president and I think most people would agree he is at least one of those two things, if not both. for 10 year anniversary of blm parade block - see notes","description":"Almost 10 years ago, Black Lives Matter held a sit in, demanding then Executive Director, @mathieu chantelois, agree to ban uniformed police officers from marching in the Pride Parade.\n\nSubsequently, when the Pride organization voted to support the ban, it created deep divisions in the city, and throughout the 2LGBTQ+ community in particular.\n\nAt the time, I took a very public stance.  My view was that there has already been so much taken away from our community, that creating deeper divisions without any clear narrative that would solve anything, was not going to be helpful for the community as a whole and likely was not even going to be helpful in addressing issues of racism.\n\nThe leaders of BLM in Toronto had just faced serious financial impropriety allegations, the primary leader had just went on a microphone publicly calling Justin Trudeau a white supremacist terrorist, amid a litany of other mayhem that might have seemed like great activism to those serving it, but to the average person not only came across as disorganized and at times violent, but also lacked any coherent talking points other than people of colour felt triggered by seeing police in uniform marching in the parade, while they stood a few feet away in full uniform on the sidelines providing city-funded protection to parade goers.\n\nAs I watched the divisions deepening by the hour, with the left launching its usual well-orchestrated politically correct rhetoric, the right firming its stance that nothing was wrong and there was nothing to see or do here except let the police back in, I looked at the situation and realized what ultimately ended up happening, which was that without a clear narrative that made sense, and without some sort of a plan to clean up the divisions, all that was happening was a division that not only fractured out community but also put us front and center to the rest of the city as a community that didn’t know what it wanted, didn’t even know what it wanted to say, and we were willing to go to extremes to be heard about it.  I’ve lost a lot in the process of being outted and coming out, I’ve also lost a lot because of the Church, and I’ve spent a long time learning about myself and learning about my community, enough to know that what Pride was doing, without much transparency, was wrong and it did not represent me.\n\nI met with one of the owners of the bathhouses that was raided, which was one of the major talking points of BLM.  He said he did not support the police being banned at all, even though he was there as the police smashed the bathhouse to pieces in front of his eyes.\n\nHe felt there had been so much work put into the relationship with the police over the years, that the ban was going to set us back in all of that and he didn’t want to see it all be for nothing.  So he and I stood in front of the pride office door one early January morning, holding a sign that said Not my Pride.\n\nWith that, things evolved into a petition of over 10,000 signatures which John Tory ignored, and the attempt to create a space where police were welcome, in or out of uniform, complete with performers from the community including several who are transgender and people of colour, who each volunteered their time to perform and show support for the police remaining in the parade.\n\nI took a lot of heat for organizing this event, there were lots of opinions about it, but my motivation was simply to say I didn’t support setting our community back like this and I am just as much allowed to try to keep things from falling apart as BLM was in trying to deconstruct them.\n\nDon’t get me wrong.  It is easy to assume that by doing this, I didn’t think there was any issue with the police and their treatment of people of colour or minorities in this city.  And it is easy to assume that I didn’t support the goal of BLM with regards to addressing racism and systemic inequalities that are not faced by white and white-passing people (such as myself, even as an Indigenous person, that looks white).  But both are entirely untrue.\n\nIn fact, if the tactics of BLM were not so clearly divisive and held even some iota of a way of uniting people and a narrative that made sense, unformed police triggering a ban necessity not remotely meeting the level of common sense that the vast majority of the city and even many people of colour, felt was necessary to take such drastic steps, then they probably would have had my support.\n\nI wrote to BLM and requested and offered to meet with them, their response was to attack me on Twitter.  I filed a human rights complaint and they attacked that.  It’s fine, there was a certain amount of optics to the entire situation that required arguments to be spelled out clearly since BLM’s script kept changing whenever common sense poked through and their actions weren’t aligning with what they were saying.\n\nBut I think with better leadership they actually could have accomplished quite a bit.  As I said at the time, and I reiterate today, I hope I don’t know anyone who thinks that there isn’t a racism problem because we should all know there is one.  And a lot more work needs to be done.  It will always need to be done because so long as there are hierarchal power structures, there will exist attempts to silence and minimize marginalized groups first. \n\nAnd I have changed my views on a few things in fact, so I wouldn’t say that their efforts were entirely for naught.\n\nOne example was the proposal that a social worker should be in police cars so that we have a professional ready to diffuse situations where police are being put into situations of applying force when it may not be necessary.  At the time I felt it was financially not feasible but I’ve come to realize that it is both feasible but also a very smart idea.  And I believe it would work.  I didn’t at the time.\n\nCould I have been convinced without all the drama?  Probably. I think most people are reasonable enough to consider new ideas when they are presented properly and in this case even when they are presented improperly if the person is open to it.\n\nThe problem is that I forced myself to remain open because I was in a battle that necessitated being sure of my positions or relatively sure and ready to adapt if necessary.  But most of the public neither has the time or interest to do that so if they aren’t given a clear and compelling argument quickly, the message gets lost.\n\nI changed my views on some things.  I’m not sure how many other Torontonians did so though, Pride almost went bankrupt after these dramatic events as supporters, individual and corporate, pulled funding and money in opposition to the decision to ban the police.\n\nI was also looking at some of the cases in the US where people of colour were being savagely beaten by police and the reactions of organizations of BLM, which in fairness really ought know no limits in terms of a response.  If people who aren’t people of colour were living in a community that was being routinely and systemically being treated like this, the response should be and must be harsh and swift.\n\nBut the response can’t turn into financial theft, nor calling our Prime Minister a white supremacist terrorist, when at that same time Donald Trump was president and I think most people would agree he is at least one of those two things, if not both.  That kind of rhetoric only stood to hurt the message, which is probably why the leader who said it was removed shortly after.  But the requirement to ban uniformed police officers was also an incoherent and nonsensical narrative that to this day doesn’t make sense even to many of the people who once yelled it from the roof tops.\n\nThe limitations that opposition and protestors should face should come from within, from organized leadership that is prepared to deliver a message that people can buy into and that unites people behind a common purpose with clear and logical calls to action.  BLM lacked that but had garnered the power to create chaos, something its leaders clearly acknowledged was part of the plan, but without one as to what to do with that power.  Creating chaos does little of all that’s left when the excitement
